High-dimensional and temporal traffic requires more than one fleet-wide scalar. Marginal feature monitors can remain flat while correlations, sequence structure, or a small cohort changes. A production design therefore combines cheap per-feature alarms with embedding- or kernel-space tests, cohort slices, rolling windows at several durations, and change-point checks. Where these statistics run, on each replica or centrally from exported samples or sketches, sets both the telemetry load on the C\(^3\) communication axis and the evidence the detector sees. The windows must preserve seasonality and event context: comparing Monday traffic with Sunday traffic can manufacture an alert, while averaging a week can hide an abrupt one-hour failure. Monitor design is part of the hypothesis, not a neutral wrapper around the model.

↩︎ The fleet monitoring hierarchy that decides where drift statistics run and which signals reach an owner is established in Hierarchical monitoring architecture.

Shalev-Shwartz, S. 2012. “Online Learning and Online Convex Optimization.” Foundations and Trends in Machine Learning 4 (2): 107–94. https://doi.org/10.1561/2200000018.
Kirkpatrick, J., R. Pascanu, N. Rabinowitz, J. Veness, G. Desjardins, A. A. Rusu, K. Milan, et al. 2017. “Overcoming Catastrophic Forgetting in Neural Networks.” Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences 114 (13): 3521–26. https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1611835114.

When the Level 3 rules do call for a model update, the update itself is a budgeted response rather than an automatic retraining command. Online learning updates a model from sequential observations (Shalev-Shwartz 2012); preserving earlier behavior remains a separate design objective. The adaptation budget depends on model size, drift rate, and feedback latency: updating too aggressively can chase noise, while updating too slowly lets performance drift. In production, online-learning systems usually bound update frequency, state size, and serving latency explicitly rather than assuming adaptation is free. An update that fits the new distribution can also erase behavior the old one required, the catastrophic forgetting that also constrains on-device adaptation. Techniques like Elastic Weight Consolidation reduce it by penalizing changes to parameters important for previous tasks (Kirkpatrick et al. 2017).

↩︎ Catastrophic forgetting and the continual-adaptation techniques that limit it are established in Continual adaptation and catastrophic forgetting.

Gama, João, Indrė Žliobaitė, Albert Bifet, Mykola Pechenizkiy, and Abdelhamid Bouchachia. 2014. “A Survey on Concept Drift Adaptation.” ACM Computing Surveys 46 (4): 1–37. https://doi.org/10.1145/2523813.

Adaptive ensemble methods maintain multiple models or hypotheses and weight or select among them using recent performance, making them useful under gradual concept drift (Gama et al. 2014). This approach trades extra serving and monitoring complexity for the ability to respond when a single static model no longer matches the deployment distribution. When the drifted data cannot leave the devices or regions that produce it, the same update can run as federated learning (Federated Learning Algorithms), which moves the adaptation to the data. The drift response then pays in communication rounds and transmitted bytes as well as training work, so the time from a confirmed shift to a validated update must be measured on the federated path rather than assumed from centralized retraining.

14.4.4 Per-request competence and selective prediction

The drift monitors and adaptation paths above ask whether traffic has changed in aggregate, yet a stable daily PSI can coexist with a rare, dangerous out-of-distribution request, while a large population shift can leave individual predictions well supported. Because a serving system must decide on every request whether that particular input lies within the model’s demonstrated competence, fleet robustness needs a per-request decision contract alongside its population monitors.

That contract combines three distinct signals because no single score answers the whole question. Calibration tests whether a score of 0.8 corresponds to approximately 80 percent correctness on a defined validation population (Guo et al. 2017). Out-of-distribution detection scores how unlike the request is from the model’s validated input distribution (Hendrycks and Gimpel 2017), because a calibrated classifier can remain confidently wrong on unfamiliar inputs. Selective prediction then turns both signals into an action policy, answering above the acceptance threshold, routing uncertain cases to a stronger model or reviewer, and abstaining when neither path can satisfy the risk bound (figure 6), since an OOD detector can flag novelty without estimating task error.

Guo, Chuan, Geoff Pleiss, Yu Sun, and Kilian Q. Weinberger. 2017. “On Calibration of Modern Neural Networks.” International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML) 70: 1321–30.
Hendrycks, Dan, and Kevin Gimpel. 2017. “A Baseline for Detecting Misclassified and Out-of-Distribution Examples in Neural Networks.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

Let \(\SelectiveAcceptanceScore(\ModelInput)\) be an acceptance score whose larger values indicate stronger evidence for answering, \(\ResponseThreshold\) its threshold, \(\ModelPredictor(\ModelInput)\) the prediction, and \(\LossFunction(\ModelPredictor(\ModelInput),\ExampleLabel)\) its loss against the observed outcome. For accepted set \(\AcceptedInputSet = \{\ModelInput: \SelectiveAcceptanceScore(\ModelInput) \ge \ResponseThreshold\}\) at threshold \(\ResponseThreshold\), coverage is \(\Pr(\ModelInput\in \AcceptedInputSet)\) and selective risk is the mean loss conditioned on acceptance:

\[ \operatorname{coverage}(\ResponseThreshold)=\Pr(\ModelInput\in \AcceptedInputSet), \qquad \operatorname{risk}(\ResponseThreshold)=\mathbb{E}[\LossFunction(\ModelPredictor(\ModelInput),\ExampleLabel)\mid \ModelInput\in \AcceptedInputSet]. \]

Sweeping \(\ResponseThreshold\) produces a risk-coverage curve rather than one accuracy number. Raising the threshold reduces coverage, but lowers accepted-request risk only when the score orders requests by error risk well enough. That relationship must be measured on the deployment population, and the rejected traffic does not disappear. The fallback path needs enough queue, reviewer, or secondary-model capacity to absorb the abstention rate within the original latency and safety budget. If labels arrive days later, the system can monitor scores and fallback load immediately but cannot confirm selective risk until outcomes return. During that delay, rate limits and conservative thresholds can limit exposure, while the default response still requires validation against the application’s hazards.

Finite-sample coverage with conformal prediction

Calibration describes whether predicted probabilities match empirical frequencies, whereas selective prediction chooses which requests the system will answer. Conformal prediction provides a different contract: it wraps a fitted predictor with a nonconformity score \(\NonconformityScore(\ModelInput,\ExampleLabel)\) and a held-out calibration set. Here the score measures how poorly a candidate outcome \(\ExampleLabel\) agrees with input \(\ModelInput\), such as one minus the softmax probability assigned to class \(\ExampleLabel\), so larger values mean less conformity and make this score distinct from the acceptance score above. On the \(\CalibrationExampleCount\) held-out calibration examples, the true label \(\ExampleLabel_i\) is known, so the system evaluates \(\NonconformityScore(\ModelInput_i,\ExampleLabel_i)\) once per example at its true outcome to measure how unusual true labels look under the fitted model. At serving time, the true label for a new input \(\ModelInput\) is unknown, so the system trials every possible candidate label \(\ExampleLabel\) in \(\NonconformityScore(\ModelInput,\ExampleLabel)\) and retains every class whose score does not exceed the cutoff calibrated on the held-out examples. For a skin-lesion classifier, the resulting prediction set might contain both benign and malignant when neither diagnosis looks sufficiently nonconforming, rather than force a single guess. The target miscoverage rate is \(\ConformalMiscoverageRate\). For the finite threshold below, assume \(1/(\CalibrationExampleCount+1) \leq \ConformalMiscoverageRate < 1\). For \(\CalibrationExampleCount\) calibration scores sorted in ascending order, set \(\ConformalQuantileRank=\min\{\lceil(\CalibrationExampleCount+1)(1-\ConformalMiscoverageRate)\rceil,\CalibrationExampleCount\}\) and \(\ConformalScoreQuantile=\NonconformityScore_{(\ConformalQuantileRank)}\). The prediction set is

\[ \ConformalPredictionSet(\ModelInput)=\{\ExampleLabel:\NonconformityScore(\ModelInput,\ExampleLabel)\leq \ConformalScoreQuantile\}, \qquad \Pr\!\left(\ExampleLabel_{n+1}\in \ConformalPredictionSet(\ModelInput_{n+1})\right)\geq 1-\ConformalMiscoverageRate. \]

Here the test pair \((\ModelInput_{n+1},\ExampleLabel_{n+1})\) follows the \(\Glyph{n}=\CalibrationExampleCount\) calibration examples. Exchangeability means that permuting their order does not change their joint distribution; i.i.d. sampling is sufficient. Under this assumption and the stated range of \(\ConformalMiscoverageRate\), the \((\CalibrationExampleCount+1)\) rank correction makes this a finite-sample marginal coverage guarantee. Exchangeability makes the test point’s score equally likely to take any of the \(\CalibrationExampleCount+1\) ranks among the combined scores. When averaging over the calibration sample and the new test example, the set therefore contains the true outcome at least at the target rate (Lei et al. 2018). Using the ordinary empirical \((1-\ConformalMiscoverageRate)\) quantile instead can undercover at finite sample sizes. The guarantee is not conditional on a particular realized calibration set, input, or subgroup. A rare subgroup can have poor coverage even when aggregate coverage is correct, particularly when it contributes few calibration examples.

Lei, Jing, Max G’Sell, Alessandro Rinaldo, Ryan J. Tibshirani, and Larry Wasserman. 2018. “Distribution-Free Predictive Inference for Regression.” Journal of the American Statistical Association 113 (523): 1094–111. https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.2017.1307116.

The systems obligation is to preserve the conditions behind the guarantee. A distribution change between calibration and serving can invalidate the exchangeability argument; calibration age alone does not establish such a change. Even if observed coverage remains adequate, the original guarantee needs its assumptions rechecked. Repeatedly tuning the model, score, or threshold against the same calibration outcomes makes the held-out evidence adaptive rather than independent. Production systems therefore version the predictor, score, calibration snapshot, and target \(\ConformalMiscoverageRate\) together; monitor realized coverage by consequential slice once labels arrive; and refresh or quarantine the contract when traffic changes. Conformalization does not protect against arbitrary shift.

Set size or interval width supplies an operational load signal. Larger outputs convey less specific predictions and can trigger abstention, a stronger model, or human review, so their tail distribution determines fallback capacity just as the risk-coverage curve determines rejected traffic. The target coverage is therefore not free: increasing it usually widens sets, raises review demand, or reduces the fraction of requests on which the system can act automatically.

The failure modes above arise without an adversary, but the boundary with deliberate manipulation is porous. Distribution monitors can themselves be evaded by inputs crafted to stay under their thresholds, turning a defensive tool into a blind spot. Once an adversary stops relying on natural variation and begins optimizing against the model’s decision boundaries to force specific errors, the threat model moves from environmental robustness to input-level attacks.

Checkpoint 14.2: Drift detection and the retraining decision

Environmental shifts split into distinct types, and the chapter’s drift framework deliberately separates a fired metric from a retraining command.

Distinguishing the shift type

Reasoning about thresholds and cost

Self-Check: Question

  1. A credit scoring model encounters a scenario where loan applicants’ demographic and financial feature distributions \(p(x)\) remain unchanged, yet default rates increase due to a sudden macroeconomic policy change that alters the relationship between income and repayment probability \(p(y \mid x)\). Which type of environmental shift does this represent?

    1. Concept drift, because the conditional relationship \(p(y \mid x)\) changed while the input marginal \(p(x)\) stayed constant.
    2. Covariate shift, because input feature values moved relative to the training distribution.
    3. Label smoothing, because target probabilities were regularized during training.
    4. Hardware bit corruption, because inference activations deviated from ground truth.

  2. A monitoring service evaluates an input feature over 1,000,000 requests, reporting a Kolmogorov-Smirnov test \(p\)-value of \(10^{-5}\) (\(p < 0.001\)), but the calculated Population Stability Index (PSI) is 0.04 and model precision is unaffected. Explain why the team should continue monitoring rather than triggering an immediate retraining job.

  3. A fraud detection team uses service-calibrated drift alerts. Which scenario most strongly warrants urgent investigation and containment, with retraining considered after the cause and candidate update are validated?

    1. A non-predictive logging metadata feature exhibits \(\text{PSI} = 0.28\) while all predictive features show \(\text{PSI} < 0.05\).
    2. A primary feature shows \(\text{PSI} = 0.06\) and \(\mathcal{D}_{\text{KL}} = 0.03\), with downstream precision and recall holding steady.
    3. A two-sample KS test on a continuous feature yields \(p = 0.01\) over \(10^6\) samples, but \(\text{PSI} = 0.08\) and accuracy is unchanged.
    4. A critical predictive feature crosses \(\text{PSI} = 0.29\) accompanied by a confirmed 7 percent drop in production precision.

  4. Order these stages of a drift investigation that considers a validated model update, while treating urgent containment as a separate response when needed: (1) Compute drift metrics against versioned reference distributions, (2) Compare the signal with available predictive and business outcomes, (3) Ingest and log production features and outcomes, (4) Validate a candidate response and apply it only if the evidence and release gate support it, (5) Investigate pipeline faults, environmental changes, and temporary events.

  5. True or False: If PSI, KL divergence, and two-sample KS tests on every input feature remain within green thresholds for six months, the engineering team can guarantee that no concept drift has occurred without checking ground-truth labels.

  6. A triage classifier answers every request whose calibrated confidence exceeds a threshold and routes the rest to clinician review. The team proposes raising the threshold to cut errors on answered requests. Explain what this change trades, what must be true of the score for it to help, and which capacity it consumes.

See Answers →

14.5 Adversarial Attacks

In the skin-lesion classifier of section 14.4.4.1, an attacker who modifies an image of a benign lesion can induce a malignant prediction while preserving its apparent clinical content. Whether that manipulation succeeds depends on the classifier, the allowed modifications, and the evaluation conditions. The high-dimensional decision boundaries learned by deep neural networks possess counterintuitive blind spots, and adversarial attacks expose them by testing what an adversary can force when controlling gradients, queries, physical sensors, or training data.

Definition 14.3: Adversarial attack

Adversarial attack is a deliberate manipulation of a model’s input, interaction, or environment designed to violate a specified behavioral objective. The manipulation may be norm-bounded and visually subtle, but it can also be semantic, physical, query-based, or conspicuous.

  1. Significance: Adversarial evaluation asks whether an attacker can choose inputs or transformations that drive the model outside its intended behavioral envelope. High-dimensional, norm-bounded image perturbations are one important case, but patches, rotations, printed objects, malicious text, and repeated black-box queries expose different system boundaries.
  2. Distinction: Unlike random corruption sampled independently of the target, an adversarial manipulation is selected to cause failure. It need not use gradients: attackers may use surrogate models, decision-only queries, search, semantic edits, or control over the physical sensing environment.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that adversarial vulnerability is a “bug” to be patched. In reality, it is a structural vulnerability of many standard neural networks trained by empirical risk minimization; robust defense typically requires fundamental changes to the objective function (for example, adversarial training).

When an attacker can inspect a model’s gradients directly, a change no person can see is enough to fool an image classifier. A perturbation that follows the sign of the loss gradient (\(\AdversarialPerturbationBudget = 0.007\)), using the fast gradient sign method defined in section 14.5.1, flips GoogLeNet’s prediction (figure 7) from panda (57.7 percent confidence) to gibbon (99.3 percent confidence). The classifier is left paradoxically more confident in the erroneous label than in the ground truth.

A visually small change need not produce a small change in a classifier’s score.7 Adversarial perturbations exploit how input changes accumulate in the model’s decision function. Even a linear function can be sensitive to many small, aligned changes across input dimensions; nonlinearity alone does not explain the vulnerability. The relevant constraint is whether the allowed perturbation can move the input across the learned decision boundary, and how an attacker finds that direction depends on the access the deployed system exposes.

7 Human vs. machine perception: First highlighted by Szegedy et al. (2013), neural networks learn statistical correlations in pixel space rather than the semantic invariances human vision enforces. Agreement on clean examples does not establish agreement under every small image change. Evaluation must therefore specify which transformations should preserve the label and test whether the model preserves that behavior; gradient-based training alone neither explains every attack nor guarantees the required invariance.

Szegedy, Christian, Wojciech Zaremba, Ilya Sutskever, Joan Bruna, Dumitru Erhan, Ian Goodfellow, and Rob Fergus. 2013. “Intriguing Properties of Neural Networks.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1312.6199.

14.5.1 Attacks by attacker access

An attacker who can read a model’s weights and gradients can flip a prediction in a single backward pass, whereas an attacker facing a rate-limited API must spend thousands of queries or train a local surrogate model to find a boundary-crossing input. The useful axis is therefore not the attack name by itself, but the attacker’s access. Each mechanism reveals a different cost that the defense must raise: gradient access, optimization time, surrogate-model construction, or physical control over the sensor environment.

The most direct case is white-box gradient access. Neural networks compute gradients to learn how parameter changes reduce loss; an attacker with access to gradients can run that logic against the input instead. In the GoogLeNet panda example of figure 7, the gradient with respect to the input image gives a local, first-order estimate of how pixel changes affect the loss. The fast gradient sign method (FGSM) turns that idea into a single-step attack by choosing each feature’s perturbation sign to maximize that first-order increase within a coordinate-wise budget.8

8 Fast gradient sign method (FGSM): Proposed by Goodfellow et al. (2015) at ICLR, FGSM generates adversarial examples in a single gradient step, making it practical to use during training. This dual role is its lasting systems significance: the same attack that exposed neural network fragility became a simple adversarial-training primitive, where FGSM-generated perturbations augment clean batches with examples chosen to increase loss.

Goodfellow, I. J., J. Shlens, and C. Szegedy. 2015. “Explaining and Harnessing Adversarial Examples.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

FGSM computes the adversarial example in one step:

\[ \AdversarialExample= \ModelInput+ \AdversarialPerturbationBudget \cdot \text{sign}\big(\nabla_\ModelInput\LossFunction(\ModelParameters, \ModelInput, \ExampleLabel)\big) \]

In this formulation, \(\ModelInput\) denotes the original input, \(\AdversarialExample\) is the generated adversarial example, and \(\LossFunction(\ModelParameters, \ModelInput, \ExampleLabel)\) represents the prediction loss evaluated at model parameters \(\ModelParameters\) and true label \(\ExampleLabel\). The gradient term \(\nabla_\ModelInput\LossFunction\) describes local loss sensitivity, \(\text{sign}(\cdot)\) extracts each component’s sign to maximize the linearized loss within the coordinate-wise budget, and the scalar \(\AdversarialPerturbationBudget\) bounds the maximum allowable perturbation magnitude under the \(\ell_\infty\) norm. This single step need not maximize the nonlinear loss or cause misclassification. Image implementations also clip the perturbed input to its valid pixel range. The subscript distinguishes this adversarial radius from other uses of \(\Glyph{\epsilon}\) in the book, such as the differential-privacy budget.

Geometrically, moving along the sign vector of \(\nabla_\ModelInput\LossFunction(\ModelParameters, \ModelInput, \ExampleLabel)\) carries input \(\ModelInput\) to a corner of the bounding cube defined by the \(\ell_\infty\) budget \(\AdversarialPerturbationBudget\), producing \(\AdversarialExample\). In the illustration of figure 8, that corner lies across the decision boundary in the adjacent class region. A different boundary or gradient need not produce a successful attack within the same budget.

FGSM is cheap because it takes one step. The projected gradient descent (PGD) attack (Madry et al. 2018) spends more compute for a stronger attack: it repeatedly applies gradient updates and projects each step back into the allowed norm ball around the original input. That iterative refinement makes PGD a standard white-box robustness benchmark. The Jacobian-based saliency map attack (JSMA) (N. Papernot et al. 2016) uses the Jacobian to identify the most influential input features and perturb a smaller set of dimensions toward a target class. These methods are most effective in white-box settings,9 where the attacker knows the model architecture and gradients.

Papernot, N., P. McDaniel, S. Jha, M. Fredrikson, Z. Berkay Celik, and A. Swami. 2016. “The Limitations of Deep Learning in Adversarial Settings.” 2016 IEEE European Symposium on Security and Privacy (EuroS&P), 372–87. https://doi.org/10.1109/eurosp.2016.36.

9 White-box attack: Adversarial attack with complete model knowledge (architecture, weights, gradients); methods like PGD and Carlini-Wagner (C&W) are strong because they optimize directly against the model rather than probing it from the outside. Though less realistic than black-box scenarios for many deployed systems, white-box analysis establishes a demanding benchmark within a specified norm, perturbation budget, and attack procedure. Passing PGD is evidence for that threat model, not a universal guarantee against every weaker-looking attack.

10 Carlini and Wagner (C&W) attack: Proposed in 2016 (IEEE S&P 2017), C&W formulates adversarial example generation as a constrained optimization problem that searches for a small perturbation causing misclassification without guaranteeing a global minimum. Its significance is methodological: C&W broke defensive distillation (an early defense that masked gradients to blunt weaker attacks) and other evaluated defenses, establishing the principle that robustness claims must be evaluated against optimization-based attacks, not merely gradient-sign heuristics.

When the attacker cares less about speed and more about stealth, the attack becomes an optimization problem. The Carlini and Wagner (C&W) attack10 (Carlini and Wagner 2017) searches for a small perturbation that causes misclassification under a chosen distance measure. Minimizing that distance encourages similarity, but does not certify human perceptual equivalence or a globally minimal perturbation. Instead of merely following the sign of a gradient, C&W optimizes a custom objective that trades perturbation size against confidence in the wrong answer.

C&W perturbations can be visually subtle, but human imperceptibility alone does not establish whether a detector will identify them. The attack can search under different norm constraints, such as \(\ell_2\) or \(\ell_\infty\). The elastic net attack to DNNs (EAD) adds elastic net regularization, combining \(\ell_1\) and \(\ell_2\) penalties to generate sparse, localized perturbations. DeepFool (Moosavi-Dezfooli et al. 2015) takes a geometric route to the same goal: it linearizes the classifier around the input, steps to the nearest boundary of that linear approximation, and repeats until the label flips, giving a fast estimate of the smallest perturbation that suffices. These optimization-based methods are more computationally intensive than gradient-sign attacks, but they give the attacker finer control over the adversarial example’s geometry.

Moosavi-Dezfooli, Seyed-Mohsen, Alhussein Fawzi, and Pascal Frossard. 2015. “DeepFool: A Simple and Accurate Method to Fool Deep Neural Networks.” 2016 IEEE Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (CVPR) 2016: 2574–82. https://doi.org/10.1109/cvpr.2016.282.

Black-box attackers lose direct gradient access but keep two routes. Transfer attacks rely on transferability, the tendency of an adversarial example to fool models other than the one it was crafted on. The attacker crafts examples offline on a surrogate model and submits them to the target, typically through its public API, without seeing its weights or gradients. Query-based attacks probe the target itself. A decision-based, or boundary, attack needs only the returned label. It starts from an input the model already misclassifies and walks along the decision boundary toward the original input, querying at each step to stay on the wrong side. Its cost is the number of queries, which is why query budgets and rate limits belong to the defense.

↩︎ The adversary knowledge spectrum, from white-box gradient access to black-box attackers who work through a surrogate or a public interface, is established in Threat vector classification.

Tramèr, Florian, Alexey Kurakin, Nicolas Papernot, Ian Goodfellow, Dan Boneh, and Patrick McDaniel. 2017. “Ensemble Adversarial Training: Attacks and Defenses.” International Conference on Learning Representations abs/1705.07204.

Transfer success depends on model similarity, training-data overlap, and regularization, and attackers can improve it with input diversity, such as random resizing or cropping, and with momentum during optimization. Closing that route costs the defender training work. Ensemble adversarial training trains against perturbations generated on several models to improve transfer robustness, but the extra attack generation and model diversity make it a deliberate training-budget decision rather than a free mitigation (Tramèr et al. 2017).

An example crafted on a surrogate fools similar models more often than dissimilar ones.

14.5.2 Physical-world attacks

White-box and black-box digital attacks modify pixel tensors directly in memory, whereas an attacker in the field cannot edit the camera buffer and must place the perturbation on physical objects such as road signs, clothing, or 3D-printed surfaces. Adversarial patches are printed patterns placed in the scene so that cameras and detectors misread it even after the pattern passes through sensors, distance, lighting, viewing angle, and ordinary deployment variation. The modification need not be visually imperceptible, because graffiti-style markings and sticker patches that pass for ordinary vandalism can still induce misclassification through the camera pipeline (Eykholt et al. 2017). The robustness question therefore becomes operational rather than purely mathematical, because a defense that works on a saved image may fail once the attack passes through a camera lens, compression, motion blur, and changing illumination. These threats matter most for AI systems deployed in physical spaces, such as autonomous vehicles, drones, and surveillance systems, where a model error becomes a safety, security, and accountability problem in the world.

Eykholt, Kevin, Ivan Evtimov, Earlence Fernandes, Bo Li, Amir Rahmati, Chaowei Xiao, Atul Prakash, Tadayoshi Kohno, and Dawn Song. 2017. “Robust Physical-World Attacks on Deep Learning Models.” ArXiv Preprint abs/1707.08945 (July).

Example 14.1: The stop sign attack
Scenario: The road-sign sticker attack, already familiar as a security threat, places black and white stickers on a stop sign so that a vision model reads it as a speed limit sign (figure 9) (Eykholt et al. 2018). The robustness question is what its two reported success rates say about how an evaluation must be run.

Diagnosis: The attack succeeded on every tested laboratory image but on 84.8 percent of frames in the moving-vehicle field test. Each number is evidence only for its own capture conditions, and the gap between them does not isolate any one factor, such as distance, viewing angle, lighting, or motion.

Systems lesson: A physical attack must be evaluated through the full sensor loop, not on saved benchmark images. The evaluation should optimize the attack over the expected range of transformations (expectation over transformation) and then test it with real captures. Any claimed mitigation, such as spatio-temporal validation or sensor fusion, must be tested against attacks on the complete perception stack.

↩︎ The road-sign sticker attack is established as a security threat in Model-Specific Attack Vectors.

The attacker’s access determines the evaluation a defense must survive. Gradient access, optimization, surrogate transfer, and physical sensor manipulation each call for their own evaluation (table 6), so a result under one attack mechanism does not become a claim about all of them.

Medical-imaging attacks can cause misdiagnosis (Tsai et al. 2023), while fraud and trading models face analogous manipulation risks. The common vulnerability is reliance on features that remain predictive on clean data but fail under crafted perturbations (Goodfellow et al. 2015; Madry et al. 2018). Defense shifts the resource cost: adversarial training adds training work (Bai et al. 2021), while runtime detection such as feature squeezing adds inference evaluations (Xu et al. 2018). The choice among these spending points motivates the defenses in section 14.6.

Tsai, Min-Jen, Ping-Yi Lin, and Ming-En Lee. 2023. “Adversarial Attacks on Medical Image Classification.” Cancers 15 (17): 4228. https://doi.org/10.3390/cancers15174228.
Self-Check: Question

  1. Why can a small gradient-directed perturbation change a high-dimensional classifier’s score more than independently sampled, zero-mean noise of comparable coordinate scale?

    1. Random noise changes model parameters directly, whereas gradient perturbations only affect input metadata.
    2. Gradient-directed perturbations require visible, large-scale pixel alterations that physically overwhelm convolutional filters.
    3. The perturbation aligns coordinate changes with local loss sensitivity, while independent zero-mean changes can cancel in the first-order sum.
    4. Random noise affects only training data, whereas adversarial noise can only exist during inference.

  2. An attacker lacking access to a commercial vision API’s internal weights or gradients trains a local ResNet surrogate, crafts PGD adversarial images against that surrogate, and successfully fools the target API on 50 percent of queries. Which property of adversarial examples enables this black-box attack?

    1. Differential privacy, which guarantees perturbation bounds across diverse neural architectures.
    2. Transferability, where adversarial examples crafted to exploit decision boundaries of one model frequently deceive different models trained on similar tasks.
    3. Quantization noise, which forces all deployed models into identical weight matrices.
    4. Batch normalization collapse, which disables activation functions across distributed API servers.

  3. Which scenario represents a physical-world adversarial attack rather than a digital evasion attack or an environmental covariate shift?

    1. An attacker submits a JSON payload containing an \(\ell_\infty\)-perturbed pixel array directly to a cloud inference API endpoint.
    2. A sudden heavy snowfall changes ambient lighting and road surface reflections, reducing camera detection accuracy by 15 percent.
    3. An engineer accidentally inverts the normalization constants in a client-side image preprocessing script.
    4. An attacker places small, calibrated black-and-white stickers on a roadside stop sign that cause a vehicle camera to classify it as a speed limit sign across varying distances, angles, and lighting.

  4. The foundational single-step adversarial attack method that computes the gradient of the loss with respect to the input and perturbs features by \(\epsilon \cdot \text{sign}(\nabla_x \mathcal{L})\) is called the ____.

14.6 Adversarial Defenses

The classifier that called a panda a gibbon in figure 7 was not broken by random noise; a perturbation budget of 0.007 per pixel, spent along the gradient sign, was enough. Defending a deployed perception model against that attacker requires a threat model that names the attacker’s access and a budget for testing and mitigation. The resulting robustness claim must state what the system pays in clean accuracy, training compute, inference latency, or operational coverage.

14.6.1 Adversarial defense workflow

A team that applies adversarial training without first naming the perturbation norm and budget can multiply training compute several-fold and lose clean accuracy while leaving the actual deployment threat uncovered. Preventing that misallocation requires an adversarial-defense workflow with four ordered layers:

  1. Define the threat model: Specify the perturbation budget, attacker knowledge, and whether the attack occurs at inference time or during training.
  2. Choose the robustness budget: Decide whether to spend the budget in training, certification, input detection, or serving-time guardrails.
  3. Measure the trade-off: Evaluate both sides of the defense, because improving worst-case behavior can reduce clean accuracy, raise inference latency, or multiply training cost.
  4. Keep evaluation adversarial: Test the defense against attacks stronger than the ones used to design it.

The order matters because an uncalibrated threat model makes the budget and evaluation loop meaningless. For model-facing perturbation threats, the most direct training-time defense is adversarial training, which incorporates adversarial examples into the training process itself. The mechanism reuses the attacks of the previous section as a data generator. Each training step augments the batch with perturbed copies of its own inputs, so the model is trained on the high-loss points an attacker would choose. In the simplest form, FGSM generates the perturbations and each batch mixes clean and adversarial examples (listing 1).

In listing 1, the sign step bounds each coordinate’s change by the perturbation budget, while clipping keeps the perturbed input inside the valid input range (for example, \([0,1]\) for normalized pixels). Concatenation doubles the effective batch size. The stop-gradient operation treats generated adversarial inputs as fixed during the outer weight update, rather than differentiating through attack construction. The caller differentiates the returned loss with respect to the model parameters and performs the optimizer update. The perturbation budget \(\AdversarialPerturbationBudget\) (the epsilon argument) bounds the allowed input change, while the attack algorithm determines how effectively it searches that set; computation reuse can reduce its training overhead (Shafahi et al. 2019).

Adversarial example generation increases training time because each training step requires additional forward and backward passes through the model (Madry et al. 2018; Bai et al. 2021). Memory overhead depends on whether the implementation stores clean and adversarial examples together, recomputes perturbations, or reuses gradient information. Iterative attacks like PGD add multiple optimization steps, so the implementation must budget that gradient work; optimized variants reduce overhead by reusing computations rather than treating every attack step as a separate full training pass (Shafahi et al. 2019). A robustness recipe that increases model capacity also adds memory and training cost. The perturbation budget and training recipe require matched clean and adversarial validation, and the perspective below shows why that matching decides what the robustness penalty actually is.

Systems Perspective 14.1: Pricing the robustness penalty correctly
Suppose a standard ResNet-50 records 76 percent Top-1 accuracy on clean ImageNet, while an adversarially trained model records about 50 percent under a specified \(\ell_\infty\) attack at \(\AdversarialPerturbationBudget=4/255\), the budget conventionally used for ImageNet. Subtracting these values yields 26 percentage points, but that gap compares different models under different evaluation conditions. It is not the clean-accuracy cost of robustness.

The clean-accuracy penalty must compare standard and robust models on the same clean test set. The robustness benefit must compare those models under the same attack, perturbation budget, and attack strength. The four cells are the standard model on clean data, the standard model under attack, the robust model on clean data, and the robust model under attack. The penalty is the first minus the third, the benefit is the fourth minus the second, and the quoted pair supplies only the first and the fourth. Reporting all four cells prevents an apparent trade-off from mixing a model effect with an evaluation-condition effect.

Systems insight: Robustness can reduce clean accuracy and increase compute, but each cost needs a like-for-like baseline. A deployment gate should report clean accuracy, empirical robust accuracy, attack specification, and evaluation cost separately.

The robustness compute penalty (principle 24) quantifies this cost: PGD-style adversarial robustness can demand several times more compute per epoch than standard optimization because each batch runs inner attack steps. In Fleet Law terms (equation 1.3) the multiplier lands on \(\IronLawComputeTime\). At a fixed worker count it adds local work, but the elapsed-time penalty depends on reuse and on which term limits the step. Adding workers changes communication and synchronization costs with \(\DeviceCount\) and may not recover the original duration. Notebook 14.2 makes the multiplier concrete.

Napkin Math 14.2: The cost of defense
Problem: A team is training a robust classifier for an autonomous vehicle. Instead of the single FGSM step in listing 1, it uses adversarial training with a seven-step PGD attack (PGD-7) that searches for a high-loss perturbation inside a specified norm ball for every training batch. Following the standard PGD adversarial-training recipe (Madry et al. 2018), it trains on the adversarial batch alone rather than on the listing’s doubled clean-plus-adversarial batch. How much does this extra attack-generation work slow down the training run?

Math: In this example, generating the adversarial input uses \(\AttackStepsPgd\) additional gradient steps per training sample, each a forward and backward pass with respect to the input, before the one weight-update pass on the adversarial batch. The estimate treats each attack step as equal in cost to the standard training pass, with no reuse; it is a work model rather than a measured wall-clock slowdown. By the same accounting, the FGSM listing spends one attack pass plus an update pass over a batch twice the original size, about three standard passes in all, so PGD-7 trades a further factor of more than two in training work for a stronger inner attack.

  1. Forward/backward passes: 1 (Standard) + 7 (Attack Generation) = 8 total passes.
  2. Training slowdown: 8× under the equal-cost-pass assumption.
  3. Utility evidence: The added work must be justified by measured robust accuracy under the specified attack. Generalization to other attacks requires separate evaluation. Pricing any clean-accuracy cost requires the like-for-like comparison in perspective 14.1, not a subtraction of robust accuracy from another model’s clean accuracy.

Systems insight: In this PGD-7 work model, the team budgets 8× the baseline compute before measuring the robustness benefit or actual elapsed-time penalty. In a machine learning fleet, robustness is not a setting that can be switched on; it is a budget the team spends, and the budget must be justified by the failure consequence of the component being hardened.

For lower-risk components, external guardrails such as input filtering and output verification can cost less than training intrinsic robustness into the weights, provided adaptive evaluation (section 14.6.1.3) supports acceptable residual risk against an attacker who knows the guardrail. Passing a finite set of attacks does not prove that no bypass exists. Safety-critical paths more often justify the training and certification budget.

Certified defenses

Adversarial training provides empirical resistance that must be tested against the deployment threat model. It can generalize beyond the examples generated during training, but attack evaluation alone does not prove invariance throughout an allowed perturbation set. Certified robustness offers a mathematical guarantee: for a given input \(\ModelInput\) and radius \(\AdversarialPerturbationBudget\), no perturbation \(\|\AdversarialPerturbation\|_\PerturbationNormOrder< \AdversarialPerturbationBudget\) exists that changes the model’s prediction. This certifies local prediction stability, not that the unchanged prediction is correct. The certified radius is a provable lower bound on the input-perturbation part of the robustness margin that compression and other efficiency changes must preserve. The guarantee formalizes the intuition that a robust model limits how quickly its outputs can change as its inputs change. The model’s Lipschitz constant11 bounds that rate, so a prediction cannot change within any radius over which the largest possible score change stays below its top-class margin. Computing or constraining a tight Lipschitz constant directly across a deep network is difficult because layer-by-layer norm bounds multiply across depth and quickly become too loose to certify useful radii. A widely used technique that avoids layerwise bound propagation on high-dimensional inputs like ImageNet is randomized smoothing. Instead of classifying \(\ModelInput\) directly, it classifies the smoothed function \(\SmoothedClassifier(\ModelInput)\), defined as the most probable class predicted by the base classifier \(\ModelPredictor\) under Gaussian noise: \(\SmoothedClassifier(\ModelInput) = \operatorname{arg\,max}_\ClassIndex \Pr(\ModelPredictor(\ModelInput+\RandomizedSmoothingNoise) = \ClassIndex)\) where \(\RandomizedSmoothingNoise \sim \GaussianDistribution(\ZeroVector, \SmoothingNoiseStandardDeviation^2 \IdentityMatrix)\). Because two Gaussian distributions centered at \(\ModelInput\) and \(\ModelInput+\AdversarialPerturbation\) overlap heavily whenever \(\|\AdversarialPerturbation\|_2\) is small relative to the noise scale \(\SmoothingNoiseStandardDeviation\), averaging the base classifier over Gaussian draws analytically bounds how fast class probabilities can shift with \(\AdversarialPerturbation\), regardless of how sharp the underlying neural network is internally.

11 Lipschitz continuity: A mathematical property that bounds how much a function’s output changes relative to its input change (\(\lVert \ModelPredictor(\ModelInput) - \ModelPredictor(\AlternateModelInput) \rVert \le \LipschitzConstant\lVert \ModelInput- \AlternateModelInput\rVert\)). A small Lipschitz constant \((\LipschitzConstant)\) limits score movement but does not by itself prevent a label flip. Reducing \(\LipschitzConstant\) can enlarge the certified radius, though enforcing a low constant can trade away clean-data accuracy and add training cost.

Cohen, Jeremy, Elan Rosenfeld, and Zico Kolter. 2019. “Certified Adversarial Robustness via Randomized Smoothing.” International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 97: 1310–20.

Cohen et al. (2019) proved a tight multiclass bound for the certified radius \(\CertifiedRadius\) using a lower bound on the top-class probability \(\SmoothedTopClassProbability\) and an upper bound on the runner-up probability \(\SmoothedRunnerUpProbability\): \(\CertifiedRadius= \frac{\SmoothingNoiseStandardDeviation}{2}\left(\StandardNormalCumulativeDistribution^{-1}(\SmoothedTopClassProbability) - \StandardNormalCumulativeDistribution^{-1}(\SmoothedRunnerUpProbability)\right)\), where \(\StandardNormalCumulativeDistribution^{-1}\) is the inverse standard normal CDF. In the binary special case, where \(\SmoothedRunnerUpProbability= 1 - \SmoothedTopClassProbability\), this reduces to \(\CertifiedRadius= \SmoothingNoiseStandardDeviation\StandardNormalCumulativeDistribution^{-1}(\SmoothedTopClassProbability)\). This Gaussian-smoothing bound certifies a Euclidean (\(\ell_2\)) perturbation radius, measured in the same units as the input to which the noise is added; it does not automatically certify the other norms used in attack benchmarks. If a binary decision has top-class probability \(\SmoothedTopClassProbability\) = 0.999 under noise \(\SmoothingNoiseStandardDeviation\) = 0.5, the simplified radius is approximately 1.5 in those input units. For an image scaled to \([0,1]\), that radius bounds the Euclidean length of the whole perturbation vector. It is not directly comparable to the per-coordinate \(\ell_\infty\) budget of the robustness-penalty comparison. The radius and that budget differ because an \(\ell_\infty\) perturbation of size \(\AdversarialPerturbationBudget\) spread over \(\InputCoordinateCount\) pixels can have Euclidean length up to \(\AdversarialPerturbationBudget\sqrt{\InputCoordinateCount}\), so a per-pixel budget that looks tiny can correspond to a long Euclidean vector on a high-dimensional image.

The multiclass case rarely estimates the runner-up probability separately. Because the class probabilities sum to one, no competing class can exceed \(1 - \SmoothedTopClassProbability\), so a lower bound on \(\SmoothedTopClassProbability\) alone also bounds every competitor. Estimating that lower bound from samples turns certification into a statistical estimation problem and adds a confidence level to the certificate; an observed class frequency alone is not a certified probability bound. Certification also carries accuracy and compute trade-offs. On ImageNet, the same work reports nontrivial certified accuracy for randomized smoothing, including certification at radius 0.5, but certification requires sampling many noise vectors per inference to estimate class probabilities with sufficient confidence. The increase in inference latency makes the sample budget a deployment constraint. Two designs keep certification outside the tight serving budget. Asynchronous auditing certifies a sampled fraction of served requests offline, so the fleet learns how often its answers were certifiable without delaying any of them. A selective safety interlock certifies only the requests routed to a safety-critical action, such as a braking decision, and lets the remaining traffic take the uncertified path.

Certified defenses cover one part of the adversarial workflow. Production systems still need detection paths for suspicious inputs, mitigation strategies that change training or serving behavior, and evaluation procedures that verify which threat models remain covered.

Detection techniques

Detecting adversarial examples before they reach the model forms the first line of defense only when the signal reaches a serving decision: reject, transform, route, or audit. A population-level signal asks whether a window of current inputs still resembles the reference population. The two-sample tests of section 14.4.2.3 serve here unchanged. A KS test, or the Anderson-Darling test, a variant that weights differences in the distribution tails more heavily, can flag a traffic window whose inputs no longer resemble the reference. Such an alert does not identify an individual request as adversarial; per-request rejection requires an additional decision rule. These tests also inherit the drift monitors’ blind spot, since an adversarial perturbation can preserve each feature’s marginal distribution while corrupting the joint structure that joint-distribution or embedding-space comparisons would expose.

An attack that preserves marginal distributions can evade that signal, motivating a complementary comparison under input transformations. Feature squeezing12 (Xu et al. 2018) reduces input-space complexity through color bit-depth reduction or spatial smoothing, then compares the model’s prediction before and after the transformation. A large prediction change flags sensitivity to the transformation. It does not by itself establish malicious intent or identify which image detail caused the change; legitimate inputs can also trigger the detector.

12 Feature squeezing: In the original evaluation (Xu et al. 2018), squeezing 256 color levels to 16 or applying median filtering eliminated many adversarial examples while maintaining high clean accuracy. The same transformations can remove useful clean-input features and do not cover every attack mechanism, so adaptive attacks that anticipate the squeezer require separate validation before the defense is treated as production-ready.

Xu, Weilin, David Evans, and Yanjun Qi. 2018. “Feature Squeezing: Detecting Adversarial Examples in Deep Neural Networks.” Proceedings 2018 Network and Distributed System Security Symposium 26. https://doi.org/10.14722/ndss.2018.23198.

13 Model uncertainty (epistemic uncertainty): Uncertainty associated with limited knowledge of the predictive model, distinguished from aleatoric uncertainty in the observations. An estimate can support deferral, but it must be evaluated against actual errors and unfamiliar inputs; a low estimate does not certify competence. Repeated-pass or ensemble estimators add inference work, and any human fallback must meet the application’s response deadline.

Lakshminarayanan, Balaji, Alexander Pritzel, and Charles Blundell. 2017. “Simple and Scalable Predictive Uncertainty Estimation Using Deep Ensembles.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 30.

Another signal asks whether the model is uncertain about its own answer. An estimate of model uncertainty13 can route an input for rejection, human review, or a more robust model. Bayesian neural networks estimate uncertainty by placing a probability distribution over each weight and averaging predictions over sampled weights, so disagreement among those samples signals uncertainty. Ensemble methods compare predictions from independently trained models and use disagreement as the warning signal (Lakshminarayanan et al. 2017). Their detection value depends on whether uncertainty distinguishes harmful inputs from legitimate ones under the chosen attack. Repeated predictions add inference work, so deployments must evaluate both missed attacks and serving cost rather than assume that a more expensive estimator detects more.

Dropout, the training-time regularizer that randomly deactivates neurons (Hinton et al. 2012), offers a cheaper approximation to that weight sampling. It can be repurposed for uncertainty estimation through Monte Carlo dropout14 at inference time, where multiple forward passes with different dropout masks approximate the uncertainty distribution. Monte Carlo dropout is itself an approximate Bayesian method. Its uncertainty estimates must be validated on the deployment population rather than ranked by the original purpose of dropout. Hybrid approaches that combine dropout with lightweight ensemble methods or Bayesian approximations balance computational efficiency with estimation quality, making uncertainty-based detection more practical for production deployment.

Hinton, G. E., N. Srivastava, A. Krizhevsky, I. Sutskever, and R. R. Salakhutdinov. 2012. “Improving Neural Networks by Preventing Co-Adaptation of Feature Detectors.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1207.0580.

14 Monte Carlo dropout: Proposed by Gal and Ghahramani (2016), MC dropout reinterprets dropout as approximate Bayesian inference by keeping dropout active across repeated stochastic forward passes. The variance across predictions provides an uncertainty estimate with no architectural changes. Each additional sample adds inference work, so deployments must select the sample count against their latency and uncertainty budgets.

Gal, Yarin, and Zoubin Ghahramani. 2016. “Dropout as a Bayesian Approximation: Representing Model Uncertainty in Deep Learning.” Proceedings of the 33rd International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML) 48: 1050–59.

Evaluation and testing

Adversarial training, certification, and detection do not exhaust the options. The remaining strategies move the spending point rather than eliminating the cost. Defensive distillation (Nicolas Papernot et al. 2016) teaches a student model from the teacher’s soft labels, but its apparent adversarial protection proved vulnerable to attacks that bypassed the resulting gradient masking (Carlini and Wagner 2017). It illustrates why making an attack optimizer fail is not evidence that nearby adversarial inputs are absent. Input preprocessing spends the defense budget at serving time: image denoising, JPEG compression, random resizing, padding, and random transformations try to erase perturbations before the model sees them. Ensembles spend it through redundancy, combining models with different architectures, training data, or preprocessing paths so that a perturbation that fools one member is less likely to fool all of them.

Papernot, Nicolas, Patrick McDaniel, Xi Wu, Somesh Jha, and Ananthram Swami. 2016. “Distillation as a Defense to Adversarial Perturbations Against Deep Neural Networks.” 2016 IEEE Symposium on Security and Privacy (SP), 582–97. https://doi.org/10.1109/sp.2016.41.
Carlini, Nicholas, and David Wagner. 2017. “Towards Evaluating the Robustness of Neural Networks.” 2017 IEEE Symposium on Security and Privacy (SP), 39–57. https://doi.org/10.1109/sp.2017.49.
Mu, Norman, and Justin Gilmer. 2019. MNIST-C: A Robustness Benchmark for Computer Vision.
Hendrycks, Dan, and Thomas Dietterich. 2019. “Benchmarking Neural Network Robustness to Common Corruptions and Perturbations.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1903.12261.

Evaluation closes the budget loop: the system must measure which attacks the defense actually covers and what performance it sacrifices under controlled attack conditions. Robustness metrics quantify resilience through accuracy on adversarial examples, the distortion required to fool the model, and performance over attack budgets. Standardized benchmarks such as MNIST-C (Mu and Gilmer 2019), CIFAR-10-C, and ImageNet-C (Hendrycks and Dietterich 2019) measure common corruptions rather than malicious worst-case behavior, so their results occupy a different cell of table 1.

Empirical attack evaluation must be adaptive to the defense. The evaluator should know the preprocessing, detector, randomness, ensemble, and rejection rule. It should optimize through differentiable components and use expectation over transformation for randomness. It should test transfer and query-based attacks when gradients are unavailable, and include the complete pipeline rather than a saved model alone. Multiple random restarts and attack budgets can strengthen evidence within that threat model, but a passed PGD test proves neither that PGD found the inner maximum nor that semantic, physical, or differently normed attacks are covered. Only a certificate establishes a guarantee, and only for its stated input, norm, radius, and assumptions.

RobustBench approximates that discipline at scale with one standardized, parameter-free attack ensemble, AutoAttack, applied to every published defense under one fixed threat model, which for CIFAR-10 is a perturbation of at most 8/255 per pixel in the \(\ell_\infty\) norm (Croce et al. 2020; Croce and Hein 2020). Across the 91 dated entries (figure 10), the best accuracy under attack rose from 44 percent in 2018 to 75 percent in 2024, while the same model kept 94 percent accuracy on clean images. Even the strongest defense therefore still fails on about 25 percent of attacked inputs. The four entries at the bottom of the plot are published defenses that keep high clean accuracy yet fall below 5 percent accuracy under the standardized attack, the result an evaluation that is not adaptive to the defense can miss.

Croce, Francesco, and Matthias Hein. 2020. “Reliable Evaluation of Adversarial Robustness with an Ensemble of Diverse Parameter-Free Attacks.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2003.01690.

Adversarial training, certified defenses, and input detection address attacks on model inputs at inference time within bounded threat models. They do not establish that the model was trained on trustworthy data. If an adversary compromises the data pipeline long before the model is compiled, inference-time behavioral tests may reveal a backdoor, but cannot by themselves establish the integrity of the training pipeline. Poisoning attacks therefore demand their own class of defenses.

Checkpoint 14.3: Choosing and budgeting an adversarial defense

An adversarial defense is a budgeted response to a specific threat model. Stronger guarantees commonly change clean accuracy, compute, latency, or coverage, but the direction and magnitude depend on the workload, model, defense, and evaluation boundary.

Matching defense to threat

Reasoning about the budget

Self-Check: Question

  1. How does multi-step adversarial training (such as PGD-7) improve a neural network’s robustness against evasion attacks during inference?

    1. It approximately searches for high-loss perturbations within the threat set and trains the model to reduce loss on those perturbed inputs.
    2. It encrypts the model’s weight matrices so attackers cannot estimate loss gradients via backward passes.
    3. It removes all non-linear activation functions from the architecture, ensuring the decision boundary is strictly linear.
    4. It replaces model weights with random distributions at inference time without requiring any changes during training.

  2. In the chapter’s equal-cost-pass PGD-7 work model, one update pass and seven attack-generation passes cost eight standard passes per batch. Standard-model clean accuracy is 76 percent, and robust-model accuracy under the specified attack is 50 percent. Explain what these quantities establish and what further measurements are needed to isolate a clean-accuracy penalty and elapsed-time cost.

  3. Which statement correctly distinguishes certified robustness (such as randomized smoothing) from empirical robustness (such as PGD adversarial training)?

    1. Empirical robustness provides mathematical guarantees across all possible norm balls, whereas certified robustness is validated only against specific attacks.
    2. Certified defenses require zero additional compute at inference time, making them ideal for high-throughput microservices.
    3. A valid certificate bounds prediction stability within its stated set and assumptions; empirical attack tests supply evidence for tested cases, while randomized smoothing adds sampling cost on the inputs certified.
    4. Certified robustness is applicable only to training data poisoning and cannot defend against test-time evasion.

  4. Order the four stages of the chapter’s adversarial defense workflow: (1) Choose the robustness budget across training-time, inference-time, and guardrail layers, (2) Define the threat model by specifying perturbation bounds (\(\epsilon\)), norm constraints, and attacker access, (3) Keep evaluation adversarial by testing defenses against adaptive and stronger optimization attacks, (4) Measure the trade-off between clean accuracy, training compute, and serving latency.

  5. A real-time image moderation service with a strict 50 ms p99 latency SLA cannot afford randomized smoothing, which requires thousands of forward passes. Design an adversarial defense portfolio that fits this latency budget and explain the trade-offs involved.
